What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, um… I came in because I feel like I’m not handling work the way I normally do. They made me the acting workflow lead in the warehouse, and ever since then I’m walking in already tense, like before anything has even happened. And then little things—like SAP errors that I usually would just fix and move on from—are setting me off. I’ll snap or get short with people, and then afterward I’m thinking, “Why did I react like that?”\n\nAnd I’m missing cycle-count deadlines too, which is really bothering me because that’s not like me. I’ve always been pretty on top of inventory stuff, shipping and receiving, all that. But lately it’s like I’m stretched thin the whole day, and by the time I get home I’m just drained. I’m not even playing basketball, and I haven’t been working on my sketches either, which are usually the things I do to feel like myself. So… yeah, I guess I’m here because this whole work change is spilling over into everything, and I don’t feel like I’m coping with it well.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious or on edge when you're not at work?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to separate it, honestly. Like, when I’m physically not at work, I don’t know if I’d say I’m anxious in the same way every minute or anything. It’s more like… I’ll be home, and part of me is still kind of bracing for work, or thinking about whether I handled something wrong, or what’s waiting for me tomorrow. So I guess there’s some of that on-edge feeling, but I notice it most when I’m either about to go in or when I’m replaying work stuff after I leave.\n\nIf I’m distracted, it’s not as obvious. But if things get quiet, I can feel my shoulders still tight, like I haven’t really switched out of that mode. So… yeah, not all the time outside of work, but it does follow me home more than I’d like.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your mood, such as feeling sad or hopeless, since taking on the new role at work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t know if I’d call it hopeless exactly, but my mood has definitely changed since they put me in that acting workflow lead role. It’s like I’m not myself. I walk into the warehouse already tense, before anything even happens, and then something small like an SAP error—something I normally would just troubleshoot and move on from—sets me off way more than it should.\n\nAnd then I feel bad after, because I’ll snap or get short, and that’s not really how I like to be with people. Plus I’ve been missing cycle-count deadlines that I honestly never used to miss, so that kind of hangs over me too. By the time I get home, I’m just… drained. Not really sad in a crying kind of way, but flat, I guess. I haven’t even had it in me to play basketball or work on my sketches, and those are usually the things that make me feel normal. So yeah, my mood’s been lower and more irritable since all this started.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding certain tasks or situations at work since taking on the new role?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I have been, though I didn’t really label it that way at first. Since they made me the acting workflow lead, there are certain things I kind of drag my feet on now, especially the cycle counts, because I’m already tense walking into the warehouse and I know if something’s off it’s going to turn into this whole thing in my head.\n\nAnd with SAP errors, it’s weird because I know how to deal with them. That’s not new to me. But lately even a small error pops up and I can feel myself getting irritated before I’ve even really looked at it, like I’m bracing for it to derail the whole day. So sometimes I’ll put off dealing with it for a minute or try to do something else first, even though that probably makes things worse.\n\nThe deadlines are the part that really bothers me, because I didn’t used to miss those. I was usually on top of that stuff. Now I’ll avoid starting because it feels like one more thing I’m going to mess up, and then by the time I get home I’m so drained I don’t even want to play basketball or sketch or do anything that usually clears my head. So, yeah, I guess I am avoiding some work tasks, not because I don’t care, but because I feel tense before I even get into them.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience any physical symptoms like rapid heartbeat, sweating, or trembling when you're feeling anxious or on edge?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure, honestly. I haven’t really been paying attention to it in that specific way, like checking if my heart is racing or if I’m sweating or anything. When I say I’m on edge, I mostly notice it as just feeling keyed up, like my body is ready for something to go wrong, but I don’t know if I could point to, like, trembling or a clear physical panic-type thing.\n\nMaybe if you asked me in the moment I’d be able to tell better, but afterward I mostly remember the tension and the stress of it, not so much specific symptoms like that. I don’t think I’ve noticed sweating or shaking, at least not enough that it stood out to me.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any traumatic or extremely stressful events recently?\n\nPatient: I mean… nothing traumatic in the way I think you mean, like an accident or violence or something like that. But extremely stressful, yeah — this whole acting workflow lead thing at work has been the big one.\n\nEver since they put me in that role, I feel like I walk into the warehouse already tense, like I’m bracing myself before the day even starts. And then little things, like an SAP error that I normally would’ve handled without making a big deal out of it, just get under my skin fast. I’ll snap or get short, and then I’m thinking about it afterward like, “Why did I react like that?”\n\nAnd I’ve been missing cycle-count deadlines, which is not really like me. I used to stay on top of those. So it’s not one dramatic event exactly, it’s more like this role has become this constant pressure, and it’s been wearing me down. By the time I get home, I’m just wiped out — too drained to play basketball or even sit down and work on my sketches, and those are usually the things that help me feel like myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're detached from yourself or your surroundings when you're feeling overwhelmed?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d describe it as detached, exactly. Like, I don’t think I’ve had that feeling where I’m watching myself from outside my body or the room doesn’t feel real or anything like that — at least not that I’ve noticed.\n\nWhen I’m overwhelmed, it’s more like I get kind of locked into my own head. I’ll be there, but I’m focused on the stress of whatever is happening, and I might not be taking in everything around me clearly because I’m thinking through what went wrong or what I still have to fix. But I still know where I am, I still feel like me, if that makes sense.\n\nSo… maybe I’d need you to explain what you mean a little more, but if you mean feeling unreal or disconnected from myself or the place I’m in, I don’t think that’s really been happening.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're in a daze or spaced out when you're at work?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it a daze, exactly. I mean, there are definitely times at work where I feel mentally overloaded, like I’m staring at what needs to get done and my brain is kind of moving slower than it should, but I don’t know if that’s the same as being spaced out.\n\nLike if something goes wrong or there are too many things coming at me, I might pause longer than usual or have to re-check what I was doing, because I’m trying to sort it all out in my head. But I don’t feel like I’m gone or not there, if that’s what you mean. I’m still aware of where I am and what’s happening around me.\n\nSo… maybe a little unfocused sometimes, but I haven’t really noticed myself being in a full-on daze. It’s more like I’m stressed and trying to keep up.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in things you used to enjoy doing?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I have, honestly. Not like I hate those things now or anything, but I’ve kind of stopped doing them, which is weird for me.\n\nBasketball is the big one. I used to show up pretty regularly, and even if I was tired, once I got there I’d feel better. Lately I just don’t go. I’ll tell myself I’m going to, and then work runs through my head or I get home feeling like the whole day took everything out of me, and I just don’t make it. Same with sketching. I used to have this usual time where I’d sit down and draw, even just mess around with ideas, and now it feels like work has expanded into that space too.\n\nAnd it’s not just in my head — it’s started showing up at work too. My supervisor actually brought up the missed cycle-count deadlines during a performance check-in, which felt pretty awful because that’s normally something I stay on top of. So it’s like work is taking over my life, and then the things that usually make me feel like myself are the first things to disappear.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious most of the time, even when there's no obvious reason for it?\n\nPatient: Most of the time… I don’t know if I’d say anxious for no reason, because in my head there’s usually a reason, even if it’s just work hanging over me. Ever since they made me the acting workflow lead, I feel like I walk into the warehouse already tense, like I’m bracing for something to go wrong before anything has even happened.\n\nAnd then little things set me off more than they should. Like small SAP errors that I probably used to handle more calmly — now I’ll snap or get irritated, and afterward I’m thinking, why did I react like that? It’s not like it’s the end of the world. But I’m already wound up, so everything feels like one more thing stacked on top.\n\nIt’s also been messing with how I’m doing there. I’ve missed cycle-count deadlines that I never used to miss, and that just feeds into it more, because then I’m thinking about how I’m slipping and how people are noticing. So even when there isn’t some big obvious crisis, I’m still tense because I’m waiting for the next issue or thinking about what I didn’t finish.\n\nBy the time I get home, I’m just drained. I don’t really have it in me to play basketball or work on my sketches, and those used to help me clear my head. So yeah, I guess I do feel anxious a lot of the time, but it doesn’t feel random exactly — it feels like work has kind of followed me everywhere.\n\nDoctor: Is there anything in particular that you think is causing your anxiety, or is it more of a general feeling that's with you all the time?\n\nPatient: I think it’s pretty clearly tied to work, honestly. It doesn’t feel like this random anxious feeling that came out of nowhere. It really started getting bad after they made me the acting workflow lead.\n\nNow I walk into the warehouse already tense, like before anything has even happened I’m waiting for something to go wrong. And then when little things happen — like small SAP errors, stuff I probably would’ve just fixed and moved on from before — I find myself snapping or getting way more irritated than the situation really calls for. Then afterward I feel bad, because I know it was a small thing, but in the moment it feels like one more problem landing on me.\n\nAnd the cycle-count deadlines… that’s been a big one too, because I didn’t used to miss those. I was pretty reliable with that kind of thing. So when I started falling behind there, it made me feel like I wasn’t keeping up, and then that just made me more tense going in the next day.\n\nSo yeah, I’d say the main cause is the acting workflow lead role and everything that’s come with it. It’s like the stress from that has followed me home, too. By the time I’m done for the day, I’m so drained I don’t even want to play basketball or sit down and sketch, and those used to be the things that helped me feel normal.\n\nDoctor: Is your anxiety only present when you're thinking about work or is it also there when you're not thinking about work at all?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly tied to work, I’d say. Like, I don’t usually wake up anxious about nothing in particular and then go looking for a reason. It’s more that ever since they made me the acting workflow lead, work is kind of sitting in the back of my head all the time.\n\nWhen I’m at the warehouse, it’s obvious — I walk in already tense, like I’m bracing for something to go wrong. And then something small happens, like an SAP error, and I’ll react way too sharply. Or I’ll realize I’m behind on cycle counts, which I never used to miss, and then that makes me feel even more on edge.\n\nBut even when I’m not actively thinking, “Oh, I need to do this at work,” it’s still sort of there in the background. Like when I get home, I’m not sitting there making a checklist the whole time, but I’m drained from it, and I don’t end up playing basketball or working on sketches like I normally would. So it follows me, I guess. It starts with work, but it doesn’t stay neatly at work.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious even when you're not actively thinking about work or any work-related issues?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so, but it’s hard to separate it out, because work has kind of become the thing that’s in the background even when I’m not directly thinking about it.\n\nLike, I’m not always sitting at home going over SAP issues or cycle counts in my head word for word. Sometimes I’m just sitting there, and I can tell I’m still tense or worn out, but if you asked me what I’m anxious about right that second, I’d probably still end up tracing it back to the warehouse. Ever since they made me the acting workflow lead, I feel like I walk in already braced for something to go wrong, and then I’m snapping over little SAP errors or falling behind on cycle-count deadlines I never used to miss before.\n\nSo even when I’m not actively thinking, “Okay, tomorrow I have to fix this” or “I missed that deadline,” it’s like the stress from it is still sitting in my body. By the time I get home, I’m drained enough that I don’t even want to play basketball or work on my sketches, and those used to be things I’d do without thinking. So… yes, I guess the anxiety can still be there, but it doesn’t feel separate from work. It feels like work keeps following me around even when I’m trying not to think about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you're completely not thinking about work or anything related to it?\n\nPatient: I mean… if I’m completely not thinking about work at all, it’s less clear. I wouldn’t say I’m just anxious out of nowhere in some totally separate way. It’s more like the work stress has gotten into everything.\n\nLike, the reason I’m even here is that since they made me the acting workflow lead, I’ve been walking into the warehouse already tense, like I’m bracing myself before anything even happens. Then some little SAP error comes up — something I used to just fix and keep moving — and I’ll snap or get way more irritated than I should. And I’ve been missing cycle-count deadlines I never used to miss, which makes me feel even more on edge.\n\nSo when I get home and I’m not actively thinking about work, I’m still just drained from it. I don’t even feel like playing basketball or working on my sketches, and that’s not really like me. So I guess the anxiety isn’t completely separate from work, but the stress from work follows me even when I’m trying not to think about it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.   },

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Honestly, when I’m actually doing something I enjoy and my mind really is off work, it eases up some. Like if I can get into a basketball game or sit down and sketch and actually get absorbed in it, I’m not sitting there panicking about the warehouse.

The problem is I’m not getting to that point much lately. Ever since they made me the acting workflow lead, it’s like I’ve been walking into work already tense, already bracing myself. Then I snap over some little SAP error that normally wouldn’t have gotten to me, or I realize I’m behind on cycle-count deadlines I never used to miss, and it just wears me down.

So by the time I get home, even if I’m not thinking, “work, work, work,” I’m too drained to do the things that would actually take my mind off it. Basketball and sketching used to be automatic for me, like my reset button, but lately I just don’t have the energy to start. So if I’m truly engaged in something I enjoy, no, the anxiety isn’t really front and center — but getting there has been the hard part.

So… yeah, I guess I’m here because this whole work change is spilling over into everything, and I don’t feel like I’m coping with it well.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious or on edge when you're not at work?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to separate it, honestly. Like, when I’m physically not at work, I don’t know if I’d say I’m anxious in the same way every minute or anything. It’s more like… I’ll be home, and part of me is still kind of bracing for work, or thinking about whether I handled something wrong, or what’s waiting for me tomorrow. So I guess there’s some of that on-edge feeling, but I notice it most when I’m either about to go in or when I’m replaying work stuff after I leave.\n\nIf I’m distracted, it’s not as obvious. But if things get quiet, I can feel my shoulders still tight, like I haven’t really switched out of that mode. So… yeah, not all the time outside of work, but it does follow me home more than I’d like.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your mood, such as feeling sad or hopeless, since taking on the new role at work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t know if I’d call it hopeless exactly, but my mood has definitely changed since they put me in that acting workflow lead role. It’s like I’m not myself. I walk into the warehouse already tense, before anything even happens, and then something small like an SAP error—something I normally would just troubleshoot and move on from—sets me off way more than it should.\n\nAnd then I feel bad after, because I’ll snap or get short, and that’s not really how I like to be with people. Plus I’ve been missing cycle-count deadlines that I honestly never used to miss, so that kind of hangs over me too. By the time I get home, I’m just… drained. Not really sad in a crying kind of way, but flat, I guess. I haven’t even had it in me to play basketball or work on my sketches, and those are usually the things that make me feel normal. So yeah, my mood’s been lower and more irritable since all this started.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding certain tasks or situations at work since taking on the new role?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I have been, though I didn’t really label it that way at first. Since they made me the acting workflow lead, there are certain things I kind of drag my feet on now, especially the cycle counts, because I’m already tense walking into the warehouse and I know if something’s off it’s going to turn into this whole thing in my head.\n\nAnd with SAP errors, it’s weird because I know how to deal with them. That’s not new to me. But lately even a small error pops up and I can feel myself getting irritated before I’ve even really looked at it, like I’m bracing for it to derail the whole day. So sometimes I’ll put off dealing with it for a minute or try to do something else first, even though that probably makes things worse.\n\nThe deadlines are the part that really bothers me, because I didn’t used to miss those. I was usually on top of that stuff. Now I’ll avoid starting because it feels like one more thing I’m going to mess up, and then by the time I get home I’m so drained I don’t even want to play basketball or sketch or do anything that usually clears my head. So, yeah, I guess I am avoiding some work tasks, not because I don’t care, but because I feel tense before I even get into them.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience any physical symptoms like rapid heartbeat, sweating, or trembling when you're feeling anxious or on edge?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure, honestly. I haven’t really been paying attention to it in that specific way, like checking if my heart is racing or if I’m sweating or anything. When I say I’m on edge, I mostly notice it as just feeling keyed up, like my body is ready for something to go wrong, but I don’t know if I could point to, like, trembling or a clear physical panic-type thing.\n\nMaybe if you asked me in the moment I’d be able to tell better, but afterward I mostly remember the tension and the stress of it, not so much specific symptoms like that. I don’t think I’ve noticed sweating or shaking, at least not enough that it stood out to me.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any traumatic or extremely stressful events recently?\n\nPatient: I mean… nothing traumatic in the way I think you mean, like an accident or violence or something like that. But extremely stressful, yeah — this whole acting workflow lead thing at work has been the big one.\n\nEver since they put me in that role, I feel like I walk into the warehouse already tense, like I’m bracing myself before the day even starts. And then little things, like an SAP error that I normally would’ve handled without making a big deal out of it, just get under my skin fast. I’ll snap or get short, and then I’m thinking about it afterward like, “Why did I react like that?”\n\nAnd I’ve been missing cycle-count deadlines, which is not really like me. I used to stay on top of those. So it’s not one dramatic event exactly, it’s more like this role has become this constant pressure, and it’s been wearing me down. By the time I get home, I’m just wiped out — too drained to play basketball or even sit down and work on my sketches, and those are usually the things that help me feel like myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're detached from yourself or your surroundings when you're feeling overwhelmed?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d describe it as detached, exactly. Like, I don’t think I’ve had that feeling where I’m watching myself from outside my body or the room doesn’t feel real or anything like that — at least not that I’ve noticed.\n\nWhen I’m overwhelmed, it’s more like I get kind of locked into my own head. I’ll be there, but I’m focused on the stress of whatever is happening, and I might not be taking in everything around me clearly because I’m thinking through what went wrong or what I still have to fix. But I still know where I am, I still feel like me, if that makes sense.\n\nSo… maybe I’d need you to explain what you mean a little more, but if you mean feeling unreal or disconnected from myself or the place I’m in, I don’t think that’s really been happening.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're in a daze or spaced out when you're at work?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it a daze, exactly. I mean, there are definitely times at work where I feel mentally overloaded, like I’m staring at what needs to get done and my brain is kind of moving slower than it should, but I don’t know if that’s the same as being spaced out.\n\nLike if something goes wrong or there are too many things coming at me, I might pause longer than usual or have to re-check what I was doing, because I’m trying to sort it all out in my head. But I don’t feel like I’m gone or not there, if that’s what you mean. I’m still aware of where I am and what’s happening around me.\n\nSo… maybe a little unfocused sometimes, but I haven’t really noticed myself being in a full-on daze. It’s more like I’m stressed and trying to keep up.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in things you used to enjoy doing?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I have, honestly. Not like I hate those things now or anything, but I’ve kind of stopped doing them, which is weird for me.\n\nBasketball is the big one. I used to show up pretty regularly, and even if I was tired, once I got there I’d feel better. Lately I just don’t go. I’ll tell myself I’m going to, and then work runs through my head or I get home feeling like the whole day took everything out of me, and I just don’t make it. Same with sketching. I used to have this usual time where I’d sit down and draw, even just mess around with ideas, and now it feels like work has expanded into that space too.\n\nAnd it’s not just in my head — it’s started showing up at work too. My supervisor actually brought up the missed cycle-count deadlines during a performance check-in, which felt pretty awful because that’s normally something I stay on top of. So it’s like work is taking over my life, and then the things that usually make me feel like myself are the first things to disappear.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious most of the time, even when there's no obvious reason for it?\n\nPatient: Most of the time… I don’t know if I’d say anxious for no reason, because in my head there’s usually a reason, even if it’s just work hanging over me. Ever since they made me the acting workflow lead, I feel like I walk into the warehouse already tense, like I’m bracing for something to go wrong before anything has even happened.\n\nAnd then little things set me off more than they should. Like small SAP errors that I probably used to handle more calmly — now I’ll snap or get irritated, and afterward I’m thinking, why did I react like that? It’s not like it’s the end of the world. But I’m already wound up, so everything feels like one more thing stacked on top.\n\nIt’s also been messing with how I’m doing there. I’ve missed cycle-count deadlines that I never used to miss, and that just feeds into it more, because then I’m thinking about how I’m slipping and how people are noticing. So even when there isn’t some big obvious crisis, I’m still tense because I’m waiting for the next issue or thinking about what I didn’t finish.\n\nBy the time I get home, I’m just drained. I don’t really have it in me to play basketball or work on my sketches, and those used to help me clear my head. So yeah, I guess I do feel anxious a lot of the time, but it doesn’t feel random exactly — it feels like work has kind of followed me everywhere.\n\nDoctor: Is there anything in particular that you think is causing your anxiety, or is it more of a general feeling that's with you all the time?\n\nPatient: I think it’s pretty clearly tied to work, honestly. It doesn’t feel like this random anxious feeling that came out of nowhere. It really started getting bad after they made me the acting workflow lead.\n\nNow I walk into the warehouse already tense, like before anything has even happened I’m waiting for something to go wrong. And then when little things happen — like small SAP errors, stuff I probably would’ve just fixed and moved on from before — I find myself snapping or getting way more irritated than the situation really calls for. Then afterward I feel bad, because I know it was a small thing, but in the moment it feels like one more problem landing on me.\n\nAnd the cycle-count deadlines… that’s been a big one too, because I didn’t used to miss those. I was pretty reliable with that kind of thing. So when I started falling behind there, it made me feel like I wasn’t keeping up, and then that just made me more tense going in the next day.\n\nSo yeah, I’d say the main cause is the acting workflow lead role and everything that’s come with it. It’s like the stress from that has followed me home, too. By the time I’m done for the day, I’m so drained I don’t even want to play basketball or sit down and sketch, and those used to be the things that helped me feel normal.\n\nDoctor: Is your anxiety only present when you're thinking about work or is it also there when you're not thinking about work at all?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly tied to work, I’d say. Like, I don’t usually wake up anxious about nothing in particular and then go looking for a reason. It’s more that ever since they made me the acting workflow lead, work is kind of sitting in the back of my head all the time.\n\nWhen I’m at the warehouse, it’s obvious — I walk in already tense, like I’m bracing for something to go wrong. And then something small happens, like an SAP error, and I’ll react way too sharply. When I’m really, fully doing something I enjoy and my mind is actually off work, then no, not in the same way. It can calm down if I get absorbed enough.

But that’s kind of the problem lately — I’m here because ever since they made me the acting workflow lead, I’ve been walking into the warehouse already tense, like I’m bracing myself before anything even happens. Then I’ll snap over little SAP errors that normally I’d just handle, or I’m missing cycle-count deadlines I never used to miss, and it sticks with me.

So by the time I get home, even if I’m not directly thinking about work, I’m usually too drained to actually get into basketball or even sit down and work on my sketches. Those are the things that would normally get my mind off everything, but lately I don’t get there much. If I’m truly into it, the anxiety isn’t really there front and center — it’s more that work has worn me down before I can even start.

So yeah, I guess I do feel anxious a lot of the time, but it doesn’t feel random exactly — it feels like work has kind of followed me everywhere.\n\nDoctor: Is there anything in particular that you think is causing your anxiety, or is it more of a general feeling that's with you all the time?\n\nPatient: I think it’s pretty clearly tied to work, honestly. It doesn’t feel like this random anxious feeling that came out of nowhere. It really started getting bad after they made me the acting workflow lead.\n\nNow I walk into the warehouse already tense, like before anything has even happened I’m waiting for something to go wrong. And then when little things happen — like small SAP errors, stuff I probably would’ve just fixed and moved on from before — I find myself snapping or getting way more irritated than the situation really calls for. Then afterward I feel bad, because I know it was a small thing, but in the moment it feels like one more problem landing on me.\n\nAnd the cycle-count deadlines… that’s been a big one too, because I didn’t used to miss those. I was pretty reliable with that kind of thing. So when I started falling behind there, it made me feel like I wasn’t keeping up, and then that just made me more tense going in the next day.\n\nSo yeah, I’d say the main cause is the acting workflow lead role and everything that’s come with it. It’s like the stress from that has followed me home, too. By the time I’m done for the day, I’m so drained I don’t even want to play basketball or sit down and sketch, and those used to be the things that helped me feel normal.\n\nDoctor: Is your anxiety only present when you're thinking about work or is it also there when you're not thinking about work at all?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly tied to work, I’d say. Like, I don’t usually wake up anxious about nothing in particular and then go looking for a reason. It’s more that ever since they made me the acting workflow lead, work is kind of sitting in the back of my head all the time.\n\nWhen I’m at the warehouse, it’s obvious — I walk in already tense, like I’m bracing for something to go wrong. And then something small happens, like an SAP error, and I’ll react way too sharply. Or I’ll realize I’m behind on cycle counts, which I never used to miss, and then that makes me feel even more on edge.\n\nBut even when I’m not actively thinking, “Oh, I need to do this at work,” it’s still sort of there in the background. Like when I get home, I’m not sitting there making a checklist the whole time, but I’m drained from it, and I don’t end up playing basketball or working on sketches like I normally would. So it follows me, I guess. It starts with work, but it doesn’t stay neatly at work.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious even when you're not actively thinking about work or any work-related issues?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so, but it’s hard to separate it out, because work has kind of become the thing that’s in the background even when I’m not directly thinking about it.\n\nLike, I’m not always sitting at home going over SAP issues or cycle counts in my head word for word. Sometimes I’m just sitting there, and I can tell I’m still tense or worn out, but if you asked me what I’m anxious about right that second, I’d probably still end up tracing it back to the warehouse. Ever since they made me the acting workflow lead, I feel like I walk in already braced for something to go wrong, and then I’m snapping over little SAP errors or falling behind on cycle-count deadlines I never used to miss before.\n\nSo even when I’m not actively thinking, “Okay, tomorrow I have to fix this” or “I missed that deadline,” it’s like the stress from it is still sitting in my body. By the time I get home, I’m drained enough that I don’t even want to play basketball or work on my sketches, and those used to be things I’d do without thinking. So… yes, I guess the anxiety can still be there, but it doesn’t feel separate from work. It feels like work keeps following me around even when I’m trying not to think about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you're completely not thinking about work or anything related to it?\n\nPatient: I mean… if I’m completely not thinking about work at all, it’s less clear. If I’m really fully engaged, like actually into something I enjoy, then the anxiety isn’t the main thing right in that moment. It can fade into the background.

But honestly, that’s been harder to get to lately. I’m here because ever since they made me the acting workflow lead, I’ve been walking into the warehouse already tense, like I’m bracing for something before anything even happens. Then I’ll snap over these little SAP errors that I normally would’ve just fixed without making it a whole thing, and I’m missing cycle-count deadlines I never used to miss. That really gets under my skin, because I used to be on top of that stuff.

And then by the time I get home, I’m just wiped out. So even basketball or working on my sketches — stuff I actually like, stuff that usually helps me reset — feels like too much effort. So I guess if I’m truly absorbed in something enjoyable, no, I’m not sitting there feeling anxious the whole time. It’s more that the work stress drains me so much that I barely get to that point anymore.

It’s like I’m not myself. I walk into the warehouse already tense, before anything even happens, and then something small like an SAP error—something I normally would just troubleshoot and move on from—sets me off way more than it should.\n\nAnd then I feel bad after, because I’ll snap or get short, and that’s not really how I like to be with people. Not really sad in a crying kind of way, but flat, I guess. I haven’t even had it in me to play basketball or work on my sketches, and those are usually the things that make me feel normal. So yeah, my mood’s been lower and more irritable since all this started.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding certain tasks or situations at work since taking on the new role?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I have been, though I didn’t really label it that way at first. I was usually on top of that stuff. Now I’ll avoid starting because it feels like one more thing I’m going to mess up, and then by the time I get home I’m so drained I don’t even want to play basketball or sketch or do anything that usually clears my head. So, yeah, I guess I am avoiding some work tasks, not because I don’t care, but because I feel tense before I even get into them.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience any physical symptoms like rapid heartbeat, sweating, or trembling when you're feeling anxious or on edge?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure, honestly. I haven’t really been paying attention to it in that specific way, like checking if my heart is racing or if I’m sweating or anything. When I say I’m on edge, I mostly notice it as just feeling keyed up, like my body is ready for something to go wrong, but I don’t know if I could point to, like, trembling or a clear physical panic-type thing.\n\nMaybe if you asked me in the moment I’d be able to tell better, but afterward I mostly remember the tension and the stress of it, not so much specific symptoms like that. I don’t think I’ve noticed sweating or shaking, at least not enough that it stood out to me.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any traumatic or extremely stressful events recently?\n\nPatient: I mean… nothing traumatic in the way I think you mean, like an accident or violence or something like that. I used to stay on top of those. So it’s not one dramatic event exactly, it’s more like this role has become this constant pressure, and it’s been wearing me down. By the time I get home, I’m just wiped out — too drained to play basketball or even sit down and work on my sketches, and those are usually the things that help me feel like myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're detached from yourself or your surroundings when you're feeling overwhelmed?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d describe it as detached, exactly. Like, I don’t think I’ve had that feeling where I’m watching myself from outside my body or the room doesn’t feel real or anything like that — at least not that I’ve noticed.\n\nWhen I’m overwhelmed, it’s more like I get kind of locked into my own head. I’ll be there, but I’m focused on the stress of whatever is happening, and I might not be taking in everything around me clearly because I’m thinking through what went wrong or what I still have to fix. But I still know where I am, I still feel like me, if that makes sense.\n\nSo… maybe I’d need you to explain what you mean a little more, but if you mean feeling unreal or disconnected from myself or the place I’m in, I don’t think that’s really been happening.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're in a daze or spaced out when you're at work?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it a daze, exactly. I mean, there are definitely times at work where I feel mentally overloaded, like I’m staring at what needs to get done and my brain is kind of moving slower than it should, but I don’t know if that’s the same as being spaced out.\n\nLike if something goes wrong or there are too many things coming at me, I might pause longer than usual or have to re-check what I was doing, because I’m trying to sort it all out in my head. But I don’t feel like I’m gone or not there, if that’s what you mean. I’m still aware of where I am and what’s happening around me.\n\nSo… maybe a little unfocused sometimes, but I haven’t really noticed myself being in a full-on daze. It’s more like I’m stressed and trying to keep up.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in things you used to enjoy doing?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I have, honestly. Not like I hate those things now or anything, but I’ve kind of stopped doing them, which is weird for me.\n\nBasketball is the big one. I used to show up pretty regularly, and even if I was tired, once I got there I’d feel better. Lately I just don’t go. I’ll tell myself I’m going to, and then work runs through my head or I get home feeling like the whole day took everything out of me, and I just don’t make it. Same with sketching. I used to have this usual time where I’d sit down and draw, even just mess around with ideas, and now it feels like work has expanded into that space too.\n\nAnd it’s not just in my head — it’s started showing up at work too. My supervisor actually brought up the missed cycle-count deadlines during a performance check-in, which felt pretty awful because that’s normally something I stay on top of. So it’s like work is taking over my life, and then the things that usually make me feel like myself are the first things to disappear.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious most of the time, even when there's no obvious reason for it?\n\nPatient: Most of the time… I don’t know if I’d say anxious for no reason, because in my head there’s usually a reason, even if it’s just work hanging over me. Ever since they made me the acting workflow lead, I feel like I walk into the warehouse already tense, like I’m bracing for something to go wrong before anything has even happened.\n\nAnd then little things set me off more than they should. Like small SAP errors that I probably used to handle more calmly — now I’ll snap or get irritated, and afterward I’m thinking, why did I react like that? It’s not like it’s the end of the world. But I’m already wound up, so everything feels like one more thing stacked on top.\n\nIt’s also been messing with how I’m doing there. I’ve missed cycle-count deadlines that I never used to miss, and that just feeds into it more, because then I’m thinking about how I’m slipping and how people are noticing. So even when there isn’t some big obvious crisis, I’m still tense because I’m waiting for the next issue or thinking about what I didn’t finish.\n\nBy the time I get home, I’m just drained. I don’t really have it in me to play basketball or work on my sketches, and those used to help me clear my head. So yeah, I guess I do feel anxious a lot of the time, but it doesn’t feel random exactly — it feels like work has kind of followed me everywhere.\n\nDoctor: Is there anything in particular that you think is causing your anxiety, or is it more of a general feeling that's with you all the time?\n\nPatient: I think it’s pretty clearly tied to work, honestly. It doesn’t feel like this random anxious feeling that came out of nowhere. It really started getting bad after they made me the acting workflow lead.\n\nNow I walk into the warehouse already tense, like before anything has even happened I’m waiting for something to go wrong. And then when little things happen — like small SAP errors, stuff I probably would’ve just fixed and moved on from before — I find myself snapping or getting way more irritated than the situation really calls for. Then afterward I feel bad, because I know it was a small thing, but in the moment it feels like one more problem landing on me.\n\nAnd the cycle-count deadlines… that’s been a big one too, because I didn’t used to miss those. I was pretty reliable with that kind of thing. So when I started falling behind there, it made me feel like I wasn’t keeping up, and then that just made me more tense going in the next day.\n\nSo yeah, I’d say the main cause is the acting workflow lead role and everything that’s come with it. It’s like the stress from that has followed me home, too. By the time I’m done for the day, I’m so drained I don’t even want to play basketball or sit down and sketch, and those used to be the things that helped me feel normal.\n\nDoctor: Is your anxiety only present when you're thinking about work or is it also there when you're not thinking about work at all?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly tied to work, I’d say. Like, I don’t usually wake up anxious about nothing in particular and then go looking for a reason. It’s more that ever since they made me the acting workflow lead, work is kind of sitting in the back of my head all the time.\n\nWhen I’m at the warehouse, it’s obvious — I walk in already tense, like I’m bracing for something to go wrong. And then something small happens, like an SAP error, and I’ll react way too sharply. Or I’ll realize I’m behind on cycle counts, which I never used to miss, and then that makes me feel even more on edge.\n\nBut even when I’m not actively thinking, “Oh, I need to do this at work,” it’s still sort of there in the background. Like when I get home, I’m not sitting there making a checklist the whole time, but I’m drained from it, and I don’t end up playing basketball or working on sketches like I normally would. So it follows me, I guess. It starts with work, but it doesn’t stay neatly at work.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious even when you're not actively thinking about work or any work-related issues?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so, but it’s hard to separate it out, because work has kind of become the thing that’s in the background even when I’m not directly thinking about it.\n\nLike, I’m not always sitting at home going over SAP issues or cycle counts in my head word for word. Sometimes I’m just sitting there, and I can tell I’m still tense or worn out, but if you asked me what I’m anxious about right that second, I’d probably still end up tracing it back to the warehouse. Ever since they made me the acting workflow lead, I feel like I walk in already braced for something to go wrong, and then I’m snapping over little SAP errors or falling behind on cycle-count deadlines I never used to miss before.\n\nSo even when I’m not actively thinking, “Okay, tomorrow I have to fix this” or “I missed that deadline,” it’s like the stress from it is still sitting in my body. By the time I get home, I’m drained enough that I don’t even want to play basketball or work on my sketches, and those used to be things I’d do without thinking. So… yes, I guess the anxiety can still be there, but it doesn’t feel separate from work. It feels like work keeps following me around even when I’m trying not to think about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you're completely not thinking about work or anything related to it?\n\nPatient: I mean… if I’m completely not thinking about work at all, it’s less clear. I wouldn’t say I’m just anxious out of nowhere in some totally separate way. It’s more like the work stress has gotten into everything.\n\nLike, the reason I’m even here is that since they made me the acting workflow lead, I’ve been walking into the warehouse already tense, like I’m bracing myself before anything even happens. Then some little SAP error comes up — something I used to just fix and keep moving — and I’ll snap or get way more irritated than I should. And I’ve been missing cycle-count deadlines I never used to miss, which makes me feel even more on edge.\n\nSo when I get home and I’m not actively thinking about work, I’m still just drained from it. I don’t even feel like playing basketball or working on my sketches, and that’s not really like me. So I guess the anxiety isn’t completely separate from work, but the stress from work follows me even when I’m trying not to think about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you're doing something you enjoy and your mind is completely off work?\n\nPatient: Honestly, when I’m actually doing something I enjoy and my mind really is off work, it eases up some. Like if I can get into a basketball game or sit down and sketch and actually get absorbed in it, I’m not sitting there panicking about the warehouse.\n\nThe problem is I’m not getting to that point much lately. Ever since they made me the acting workflow lead, it’s like I’ve been walking into work already tense, already bracing myself. Then I snap over some little SAP error that normally wouldn’t have gotten to me, or I realize I’m behind on cycle-count deadlines I never used to miss, and it just wears me down.\n\nSo by the time I get home, even if I’m not thinking, “work, work, work,” I’m too drained to do the things that would actually take my mind off it. Basketball and sketching used to be automatic for me, like my reset button, but lately I just don’t have the energy to start. So if I’m truly engaged in something I enjoy, no, the anxiety isn’t really front and center — but getting there has been the hard part.\n\nDoctor: When you are doing something you enjoy and your mind is completely off work, do you still feel anxious?\n\nPatient: When I’m really, fully doing something I enjoy and my mind is actually off work, then no, not in the same way. It can calm down if I get absorbed enough.\n\nBut that’s kind of the problem lately — I’m here because ever since they made me the acting workflow lead, I’ve been walking into the warehouse already tense, like I’m bracing myself before anything even happens. Then I’ll snap over little SAP errors that normally I’d just handle, or I’m missing cycle-count deadlines I never used to miss, and it sticks with me.\n\nSo by the time I get home, even if I’m not directly thinking about work, I’m usually too drained to actually get into basketball or even sit down and work on my sketches. Those are the things that would normally get my mind off everything, but lately I don’t get there much. If I’m truly into it, the anxiety isn’t really there front and center — it’s more that work has worn me down before I can even start.\n\nDoctor: When you're fully engaged in something you enjoy, do you still feel anxious?\n\nPatient: If I’m really fully engaged, like actually into something I enjoy, then the anxiety isn’t the main thing right in that moment. Completely relaxed? I mean… not really lately, not in the way I used to think of relaxed.

There are moments where it eases up, like if I can actually get absorbed in something, but since they put me in that acting workflow lead spot, it’s like my baseline is just different. Or I realize I’m behind on cycle-count deadlines, which bothers me because I didn’t used to miss those things.

And then by the time I get home, I’m just drained. Basketball used to help, sketching used to help, but lately even starting those feels like another task. So I wouldn’t say I’m never calmer, but completely relaxed and free of anxiety… no, that’s been pretty rare.

So… yes, I guess the anxiety can still be there, but it doesn’t feel separate from work.
